Sustainable port practices and shipping operations at Onne Port, Rivers State, Nigeria: evidence from technology, energy and waste management systems

Damilare Victor Oyewale, Okpara Ruth Ada, Folorunso John Adeyi — 2026-12-31 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22345650

This study evaluates sustainable port practices and their implications for shipping operations at Onne Port, Rivers State, Nigeria, with particular attention to technology adoption, energy consumption and waste management. A survey research design was used, and primary data were collected through structured questionnaires from 100 purposively selected stakeholders drawn from shipping companies, freight-forwarding firms and shippers. Descriptive statistics were used to examine technology, energy and waste-management practices, while multiple regression tested the relationship between questionnaire-based measures of port revenue, standard of living and sustainable port operations. The findings show that digitalisation remains incomplete: 46% of respondents disagreed that technology had fully replaced manual methods, whereas 31% agreed. Thermal/hydropower and combustible fuel were the dominant reported energy sources, accounting for 44% and 39%, respectively. Landfill and incineration were the principal waste-management methods, representing 44% and 32%, while recycling accounted for only 13%. The regression model was statistically significant, F(2,97) = 51.179, p < .001, with R = 0.717 and R² = 0.513. Port revenue and standard of living were both significant predictors, and the latter showed the stronger standardised association. The study concludes that sustainable port development at Onne Port requires deeper digitalisation, cleaner energy systems, circular-economy-based waste management and stronger integration of economic, environmental and social objectives in port governance.

Technology application for managing inland water transport safety in Nigeria: a multi-state assessment of adoption and contextual suitability

Ahmad Bouchi Maik Garba, J. A. Ojekunle — 2026-12-31 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22666566

Inland water transport (IWT) is a vital mode for Nigeria’s riverine communities, yet it remains hazardous and technologically underdeveloped. While Global Positioning System (GPS) tracking, the Automatic Identification System (AIS) and real-time monitoring have improved IWT safety internationally, the extent and contextual suitability of such technologies in Nigeria are undocumented. This study assessed the application of ten safety technologies, GPS tracking, AIS, closed-circuit television (CCTV), very high frequency (VHF) radio, satellite phones, distress alert systems, communication tools, Electronic Chart Display and Information Systems (ECDIS), online ticketing and smart cards, across Lagos, Niger and Kogi States. A structured questionnaire measuring six attributes (availability, applicability, accessibility, functionality, safety contribution and flexibility) on a five-point Likert scale was administered to 512 stakeholders comprising boat operators, passengers, regulators and safety officers. Data were analysed using descriptive statistics and one-way analysis of variance. Basic, low-cost technologies ranked highest: GPS tracking recorded the highest applicability (mean = 3.45, SD = 0.94) and was affirmed as safety-enhancing by 67.6% of respondents, followed by communication tools (63.9%) and online ticketing (60.4%). Advanced systems ranked lowest, with AIS (39.6%), ECDIS (38.3%) and satellite phones (32.0%) constrained by cost, infrastructure and technical capacity. Differences across technologies were statistically significant. The findings indicate that IWT safety in Nigeria is best advanced through phased investment in affordable, contextually appropriate technologies, supported by policy reform and stakeholder capacity building, rather than through the wholesale importation of advanced systems designed for high-resource settings.

The Decoupling Mechanism as a Boundary Condition: Geometric Resolution to the Dark Sector and Hubble Tensions

Nadav Bashan — 2026-12-02 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.18674955

We test whether radiative decoupling surfaces admit a scale-independent geometric normalization that can be evaluated at stellar photospheres and projected to the cosmic horizon. By coupling independently measured radiative observables with Newtonian surface gravity, we define a dimensionless boundary invariant, \mathcal{X}. Using a benchmark sample of 190 detached eclipsing-binary (DEB) components, we find that stellar photospheres cluster near the projected blackbody phase-space capacity \mathcal{X}_0 = \pi^3/15 \simeq 2.067. While evaluating this invariant using standard macroscopic relations yields an algebraic tautology, this strict circularity mathematically demonstrates that standard physical laws inherently force mass and gravity to perfectly cancel at the radiative boundary, leaving a pure geometric invariant. Projecting this identical, mass-independent boundary normalization to the Hubble horizon yields a parameter-free prediction for the dark energy density fraction, \Omega_\Lambda = \pi^3/45 \simeq 0.6890, consistent with Planck constraints. Assuming a spatially flat universe, this boundary capacity strictly mandates a total matter density fraction of \Omega_m = 1 - \pi^3/45 \simeq 0.3110, seamlessly matching empirical consensus without fine-tuning. Finally, expressing the transition from a continuous early-universe fluid to a discrete late-universe void network as a geometric packing gap, motivated by the optimal local packing limit (k=12), yields a kinematic mapping H_0^{\mathrm{local}} = H_0^{\mathrm{CMB}}(13/12) \simeq 73.0\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}. This geometric formulation natively resolves the most persistent cosmological anomalies through pure spatial boundaries. Key Predictive Results This framework introduces a parameter-free, purely geometric formulation that natively resolves three of the most persistent anomalies in modern cosmology without the need for unobserved parameters or fine-tuning: Dark Energy (\Omega_\Lambda): Evaluated as a projected 2D horizon surface capacity rather than a 3D bulk volume density, yielding \Omega_\Lambda = \pi^3/45 \simeq 0.6890. Total Matter (\Omega_m): Derived directly from the spatial flatness constraint (\Omega_{\mathrm{tot}}=1) as the complementary geometric remainder, yielding \Omega_m = 1 - \pi^3/45 \simeq 0.3110. The Hubble Tension (H_0): Modeled as a kinematic offset reflecting the mandatory geometric packing gap between a continuous early-universe fluid and a discrete late-universe void network (based on the kissing number k=12), yielding H_0^{\mathrm{local}} = H_0^{\mathrm{CMB}}(13/12) \simeq 73.0 \mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}. Repository Contents Manuscript.pdf: The full research paper. verify_boundary.py: A self-contained, reproducible Python script that performs the empirical validations and computes the cosmological projections. data.csv: The empirical benchmark dataset of 190 detached eclipsing binary (DEB) components from Torres et al. (2010) used for the photospheric evaluation.

Geometric Derivation of the Gravitational Constant from Lambda under Radiative Boundary Normalization

Nadav Bashan — 2026-12-02 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.18715632

In this work, we present a geometric boundary framework that explicitly derives the gravitational relation (G) from the cosmological constant (Λ) through a scale-independent surface normalization. Starting from radiative decoupling surfaces, we identify a dimensionless boundary relation converging to the exact mass-independent identity π³/15. Extending this boundary condition to the cosmic horizon yields the master constraint ΛR_H² = π³/15. By incorporating holographic tiling of the horizon area, we extract an explicit algebraic expression for G, governed entirely by the discrete information bound (N) of the macroscopic surface. Within this formulation, the canonical ~ 10^120 vacuum-energy discrepancy is mathematically resolved not as an energy divergence, but as the exact dimensional scaling factor required to map the macroscopic horizon area to Planck-scale elements. Consequently, the dark-energy fraction and Hubble-scale kinematics emerge as rigid geometric imperatives of the identical boundary condition.

The Holographic Boundary Principle: A Geometric Normalization from Stellar Photospheres to the Cosmic Horizon

Nadav Bashan — 2026-12-02 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.18726496

Astrophysical observation accesses boundaries: the 2D decoupling surfaces where photons last interact with matter, not the full 3D bulk. In this Letter we treat radiative decoupling as a geometric boundary condition and define a dimensionless boundary relation by coupling standard radiative observables to Newtonian surface gravity. Under the standard macroscopic closures, reduces algebraically to a mass-independent geometric identity and approaches the blackbody phase-space capacity . We evaluate empirically using two strictly separated benchmarks. First, an independent Solar evaluation gives , consistent with a finite-optical-depth photosphere bounded below the ideal sharp-surface limit. Second, a kinematic sample of 190 detached eclipsing-binary components yields an ensemble mean with small dispersion and no trend with stellar mass, directly illustrating the predicted scale-independence of the boundary relation across diverse stellar parameters. If the same boundary capacity governs the macroscopic decoupling surface of the observable universe, projecting the normalization to the Hubble horizon gives the boundary equation . Assuming spatial flatness, this yields parameter-free density fractions and , consistent with CMB constraints. Introducing a discrete horizon state count , the boundary equation can be written as an explicit holographic mapping between macroscopic tension and microscopic gravity, . In this framework, the canonical vacuum discrepancy is re-expressed as the required dimensional area-scaling between the macroscopic horizon and the Planck area count , rather than as a mismatch of physical energy scales. Finally, modeling the late-time universe as a discretized void network suggests a purely geometric kinematic offset between early- and late-time Hubble inferences: adopting the optimal 3D kissing number gives a gap fraction and the scaling . The resulting framework contains no fitted parameters and treats key cosmological quantities as consequences of boundary geometry.

Vaccination in Children with Paediatric Autoimmune and Autoinflammatory Rheumatic Diseases: Evidence on Safety, Immunogenicity and Effectiveness Across Live-Attenuated, mRNA and Recombinant Vaccines

Mohammad Hamad Said, Infection & Immunity, Immuno/reuma patientenzorg, Joris van Montfrans, Bas Vastert — 2026-10-07 — openalex

DOI : —

Vaccination in children with paediatric autoimmune and autoinflammatory rheumatic diseases (pedAIIRDs) is clinically important but challenging. These children have an increased risk of infections due to both their underlying immune-mediated disease and the immunosuppressive therapies required for disease control. At the same time, immunosuppression may reduce vaccine-induced immune responses, while concerns about disease flares and the safety of live-attenuated vaccines may result in delayed or incomplete vaccination. This thesis aimed to generate clinically applicable, real-world evidence on the safety, immunogenicity and effectiveness of vaccination in children with pedAIIRDs receiving immunosuppressive therapy. Five studies addressed three vaccine platforms: the live-attenuated measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) booster vaccine, COVID-19 mRNA vaccines and the recombinant zoster vaccine (RZV; Shingrix). In a cohort of 186 children with juvenile idiopathic arthritis (JIA), MMR booster vaccination was not associated with increased disease activity, including in children receiving biological disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs, and no serious vaccine-related adverse events were observed. Long-term evaluation of 182 children with JIA showed that most patients maintained protective antibodies against measles, mumps and rubella. However, measles seroprotection five years after vaccination was significantly lower in patients receiving biologic therapy at vaccination than in those not receiving biologics, supporting a targeted approach to serological monitoring. A systematic review and meta-analysis demonstrated that COVID-19 vaccination was associated with a marked reduction in the risk of multisystem inflammatory syndrome in children (MIS-C). In a prospective cohort of 157 children with paediatric rheumatic diseases, COVID-19 mRNA vaccination induced humoral responses in nearly all patients and adequate T-cell responses in most tested patients. Although antibody concentrations were lower in patients receiving biologic therapy, vaccine effectiveness against symptomatic COVID-19 was preserved. No increase in disease activity, myocarditis or serious adverse events was identified. Finally, off-label use of RZV was evaluated in ten varicella-zoster-virus-naïve immunocompromised children for whom live-attenuated varicella vaccination was unsuitable. Nine of ten patients seroconverted following two doses. Two subsequently developed mild breakthrough varicella after household exposure and recovered without hospitalisation or antiviral treatment. Together, these studies support a shift from broad vaccine avoidance towards individualised, treatment-specific vaccination strategies in immunocompromised children. The findings provide evidence for the safe use of selected live vaccines, targeted monitoring of long-term vaccine-induced immunity, effective use of mRNA vaccines and further evaluation of non-live vaccine alternatives. These results may contribute to future national and international vaccination recommendations for children with immune-mediated inflammatory diseases.

A Dual-MHC-Class Multi-Epitope Polytope Vaccine against Bluetongue Virus Engineered for Multi-Receptor Innate Immune Engagement

Vallayyachari Kommoju, D. Syam Babu, T. C. Venkateswarulu, Yaswanth Naveen Kumar Alla — 2026-10-05 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22720542

Bluetongue virus (BTV) causes major economic losses in ruminant livestock. Existing vaccines are single peptide, serotype-specific, with limited cross serotype protection. Using computational tools, this study designed dual MHC class, dual peptide, multi-epitope polytopes with cross serotype protection. Epitopes were predicted from BTV VP2 and VP5 using NetMHCpan/NetMHCIIpan against bovine BoLA alleles and were filtered for conservation using BLAST across 20 serotypes. Lead epitopes, single epitope from each protein were joined by GP linkers construct MHC-I restricted Polytope1 and MHC-II restricted Polytope2. Both constructs were assessed for antigenicity (VaxiJen), allergenicity (AllerTOP), structure (AlphaFold3, Ramachandran analysis), receptor docking (ClusPro), 100 ns molecular dynamics (Desmond), bovine proteome cross-reactivity (BLASTP), and expression feasibility (in silico cloning, pET-28a (+)). Four lead epitopes 2 from each protein (MHC-I and MHC-II restricted each), were selected based on NetMHC rank, VaxiJen score, serotypes coverage. From VP2, EPI1- RTNPYPCLR (MHC-I; 0.46, 1.97, conserved in 19/20 serotypes) and EPI3, LRGALIAAETQLGDV (MHC-II; 1.16; 0.93; 16/20 serotypes). From VP5 EPI2, KQAVLLNVL (MHC-I; 0.30; 0.21; 20/20 serotypes) and EPI4, ATGRAIEGAYKLKKV (MHC-II; 0.02; 0.64; 20/20 serotypes). Re-screening against 183 pooled strain-level sequences confirmed strong conservation for three epitopes (93.1–98.8%), EPI3 showed reduced conservation (71.6%), consistent with VP2's known hypervariability. Both polytopes were antigenic (0.802, 1.026) and non-allergenic, with 100% of residues in favoured/allowed Ramachandran regions. Docking gave favourable binding energies with all receptors (ΔG -7.9 to −11.2 kcal/mol). Polytope1 was most favourable with TLR4 (-10.8) and TLR7 (-11.2), through compact interfaces (16-29 residues). Polytope 2 formed larger, contact-dense interfaces (up to 1537 Å², 207 contacts with TLR7). Polytope1 ligand RMSD stayed below 0.45 nm across all receptors over 100 ns. Hydrogen-bond counts were receptor-dependent, Polytope1 Vs Polytope2 with TLR3 (6 vs 16), with TLR7 (12 vs 10). BLASTP against the bovine proteome found no hit combining high identity with extensive coverage, indicating low cross-reactivity risk. In silico cloning confirmed feasible recombinant expression of both constructs in pET-28a (+). This in silico designed and validated a dual-MHC-class, multi-epitope BTV vaccine candidate binding to both surface and endosomal innate receptors with strong cross-serotype conservation. This combination has not been reported in prior BTV vaccine studies. These findings are purely computational and require experimental confirmation. Future work will validate immunogenicity and protective efficacy in the natural host, and test liposomal TLR agonist co-delivery. Keywords: Bluetongue virus, Bluetongue virus serotypes, Toll Like Receptor (TLR), VP2/VP5, Multi-Epitope, Vaccine

Cancer under pressure: metabolic adaptations and drug resistance

Maria Matamala Montoya, Metabolism & Cancer - Metabolomics Cell Biology, Veterinaire biochemie, Celia Berkers, Esther Zaal — 2026-10-05 — openalex

DOI : —

Cancer cells exist in constantly changing and stressful environments characterized by fluctuations in nutrients and oxygen, interactions with the immune system and exposure to anticancer therapies. To survive these challenges, cancer cells can reprogram their metabolism, switching between nutrient sources and metabolic pathways to maintain energy production, biosynthesis, and redox balance. Although this metabolic flexibility promotes tumor survival and therapy resistance, it can also create specific metabolic dependencies that represent potential therapeutic vulnerabilities. This thesis investigates how metabolic adaptation contributes to drug resistance and disease progression in multiple myeloma (MM) and colorectal cancer (CRC). Despite their distinct origins and biological characteristics, both cancers can reshape their metabolism in response to stress. A multi-omics approach integrating metabolomics, stable isotope tracing, proteomics and transcriptomics was used across drug-resistant cancer cell models, cancer–stromal cell co-cultures, and patient-derived organoids. These approaches enabled the characterization of metabolic changes and nutrient utilization under different environmental and therapeutic conditions. In MM, the thesis demonstrates the important role of the tumor microenvironment, particularly bone marrow stromal cells, in promoting resistance to the proteasome inhibitor bortezomib. Stromal cells reduced MM sensitivity to treatment and induced metabolic changes in cancer cells, including increased oxidative phosphorylation, TCA cycle activity and serine synthesis. The interaction was reciprocal, with MM cells inducing cancer-associated fibroblast-like characteristics in stromal cells and stimulating cytokine production, particularly interleukin-6 (IL-6). Elevated IL-6 was associated with metabolic alterations and reduced bortezomib sensitivity, highlighting the role of microenvironmental signaling in metabolic adaptation. The thesis further identifies one-carbon metabolism as an important metabolic vulnerability in bortezomib-resistant MM. Resistant cells showed increased serine utilization, nucleotide synthesis and mitochondrial one-carbon metabolism. Inhibition of MTHFD1/MTHFD2 with TH9619 reduced the survival advantage of resistant cells and acted synergistically with bortezomib, suggesting that targeting one-carbon metabolism could help overcome therapeutic resistance. In CRC, the thesis investigates the poorly characterized gene DHFR2 and its role in cellular metabolism. DHFR2 loss affected one-carbon metabolism, nucleotide production, and mitochondrial function, although the effects varied between cellular models and clones. These findings emphasize the context-dependent nature of cancer metabolism and suggest that DHFR2 contributes to the maintenance of metabolic homeostasis and folate metabolism. Overall, this thesis demonstrates that metabolic plasticity is a key mechanism through which cancer cells adapt to therapeutic and environmental stress. Across MM and CRC, increased mitochondrial metabolism, altered serine utilization, and dependence on one-carbon metabolism emerge as recurring features. Identifying these adaptive dependencies may enable the development of combination therapies that target metabolic vulnerabilities alongside conventional treatments, potentially improving treatment durability and overcoming drug resistance.

Goats in a Warming World: Mechanisms of Climate Resilience and Strategies for Sustainable Production

V Sejian, G Kalaighnazhal, E.Binuni Rebez, M V Silpa, I Tasmiya — 2026-10-05 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22702137

Abstract Goats are well adapted to environmental stress and possess superior thermotolerance potential that enables them to perform under extreme temperatures, limited feed and water availability and other challenging conditions. Their resilience potential is associated with physiological, behavioural and genetic characteristics that allows them to tolerate harsh conditions however, the expression of these traits differs among breeds and individual animals. Among the goat populations, indigenous goat breeds are valuable because of generations of adaptation to local environments have shaped genetic diversity that can contribute to future climate-resilient breeding programs. At the molecular level, genomics and other omics studies are providing insights into the biological and molecular processes associated with these adaptive responses. However, the ability to withstand environmental stress does not always ensure maintenance of production, since increased thermoregulatory demands can alter nutrient partitioning and reduces the resources available for growth, reproduction, milk and meat production. Nutritional and management interventions can help minimize these production losses however, genetic interventions can provide permanent solution by combining adaptive capacity with desirable productive traits over generations. Further, Precision livestock farming (PLF) technologies support this process by enabling continuous monitoring of animal behaviour, physiology and environmental exposure. Such efforts facilitate the identification and selection of goats that remain healthy and productive under climatic stress thereby, strengthening the basis for sustainable production and food security in increasingly variable environments. Keywords: Climate change; Goats; indigenous breeds; omics technologies; PLF; resilience 1. Introduction: The Goat as a Climate-Resilient Livestock Species As the global population continues to grow rapidly, there will be an increasing demand for animal-source foods to ensure the constant food supply to the growing human population. However, climate change is altering the livestock production as the conditions that would favor livestock productivity is becoming hostile. The increasing temperature, reduced availability of water, reduction in grazing land and increased climatic variability significantly impacts the productivity, reproductivity and welfare of animals (Sejian et al., 2018). Under these circumstances, livestock species that have lower requirements but remain productive will be the key players in ensuring global food securityAmong livestock species, goats have remarkable adaptation to climatic conditions as they can survive in different climatic zones including the Sahara Desert where temperatures rise above 45°C, as well as high altitude regions such as the Himalayas above 4,000 meters, tropical rainforest regions and semi-arid drought prone areas (Danso et al., 2024). They play a significant role in contributing to global food security through production of meat, milk, fiber, hides and manure, as well as being an important source of income to millions of poor farmers (Sejian et al., 2018). The ability of goats to withstand the harsh climatic changes is due to the fact that they possess unique attributes in terms of morphology, physiology, behavior and genetics. The small body size, thermoregulation capacity, browsing behavior, lower water requirement and their capability to feed on low-quality feed allows goats to remain productive under adverse and limited climatic conditions (Nair et al., 2021). The advancement in next-generation sequencing technologies and omics platforms have played an enormous role in gaining insight into the molecular mechanisms of these adaptation capabilities, which has assisted in identifying the genes and biomarkers for climate-resilient breeding (Kalaignazhal et al., 2024). Additionally, integrating with the emerging precision livestock farming enables real-time evaluation of animal health, well-being and environmental reactions, thus leading to more accurate and climate-smart management practices (Rebez et al., 2024). This chapter focuses on the effects of climatic stressors on goat production systems, as well as the biology, genetics, and molecular adaptations in goats that allows them to cope up with the challenging environments. In addition, the chapter discusses the inclusion of indigenous genetic resources, application of multi-omics approaches, precision livestock farming, and climate-smart approaches for formulating a holistic strategy for goat production. 2. Climate Stressors Shaping the Future of Goat Production Climate change is modifying the environmental circumstances that have made goat farming successful previously. The increase in ambient temperature, increased and persistent heat waves, irregular precipitation, persistent droughts and extreme weather conditions have become a common phenomenon (Sejian et al., 2018).These changes are not only intensifying thermal stress but are also affecting the availability of feeds and water, vegetation development, and the distribution of pasturelands (Nardone et al., 2010).In many arid and semi-arid areas, climate changes have contributed to desertification, decrease in the availability of food and increase in competition for natural resources.With the intensification of these climate changes even the goats withsuperior adaptive abilityare facing greater challenges in maintaining their health and productivity. The climatic stressors have significant effects on goat health, well-being and productivity. Among these stressors, heat stress is considered to be the most detrimental affecting the physiological processes by elevating body temperature, respiration rateand water intake while decreasing feed intake and nutrient absorption.Further, long-term heat exposure will adversely affect the growth performance, reproduction, milk productivity, immunity and kid survival rate (Cheng et al., 2022). At the same time, feed shortage caused by drought also results in reduced quantity and quality of pasture, thereby making the goats rely on browsing of poor-quality feed that might fail to satisfy the nutritional needs of the goats.Water scarcity further compounds these problems as it impacts metabolic functions, thermoregulationand the performance of animals.In addition to these direct effects, climate change hinders livestock production through their indirect effects by altering feed resources, grazing ecosystem and the distribution of parasites and infectious diseases (Lacetera et al., 2019).Thus, climatic factors decrease productivity and make goat farming systems more vulnerable especially in the developing countries. 3. The Biology of Goat Resilience: Why Do Some Goats Cope Better? Goat’s ability to survive climatic stress differs considerably between breeds, even among the individuals of same breed and production system (Renaudeau et al., 2012).Some goats resist and perform normally in terms of growth, reproduction, milk production and immune system functioning in hot conditions, while other goats fail to resist the climatic conditions and thus their performances deteriorate significantly. This resistance to climatic stress depends on the adaptation ability of these goats, which is influenced by genetics, environmental exposure, physiological plasticity, nutrition and adaptation to the local climatic condition (Sejian et al., 2018). Consequently, indigenous goats are usually more tolerant to climatic stress because they have been evolving for generations in either hot, dry, or mountainous climates and the generations of exposure to the unfavorable environmental condition have allowed positive selection for beneficial alleles and regulatory mechanism that help goats survive under climatic stress (Sejian et al., 2021). Therefore, climate resilience is not a single trait but rather a combination of many biological mechanisms that act together to ensure normal body functioning amid stressful environmental conditions. The adaptation of goats to climate depends on the combined effect of the physiological, behavioral, endocrine, and molecular processes to counteract the impact of environmental stress. Physiological thermoregulation through sweating, panting, vasodilation of blood vessels and metabolic heat reduction, as well as behavioral adaptation of the animals like seeking for shade and altering grazing behaviour are helpful in thermoregulation. These mechanisms are further aided by efficient water use, feeding of poor quality feedstuffs and metabolic plasticity that enable the goats to maintain their physiological functions despite being exposed to heat and nutritional stresses for a prolonged period (Nair et al., 2021). Hormonal regulation through cortisol and thyroid hormones additionally regulates metabolism in accordance with requirements of the environment, while increased expression of heat shock proteins and antioxidant enzymes ensures protection of cells against oxidative stress, stabilization of proteins, and cell viability (Nair et al., 2021). At the molecular level, these adaptation processes occur through the interplay of several genes that are associated with thermoregulation, immune system, energy balance, hydration and cell maintenance. The expression of these genes are further influenced by several factors like environmental conditions, nutrition, epigenetic mechanisms, host-microbiome relationship and management practices (Abioja et al., 2023). Consequently, these mechanisms define the climate resilience in goats and can be used as a basis for improving breeding, nutrition and farm management. 4. The Multi-Omics Architecture of Climate Resilience Climate resilience in goats cannot be comprehensively understood through research on one particular gene or biological process since each level of biology plays an important role in the adaptation process. Omics te

Construction and validation of a predictive model for the prognosis of hepatocellular carcinoma based on immunogenic cell death-related genes

GUO Cancan, LI Huanteng, MA Dongmei, GUO Hui, WANG Wei, LIU Shihai, PAN Huazheng — 2026-10-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.13362/j.jpmed.202641048

Objective To construct a predictive model for the prognosis of patients with hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) based on immunogenic cell death (ICD)-related genes, and to assess its clinical translational value. Methods A total of 365 HCC patients were selected from the Liver Hepatocellular Carcinoma (LIHC) cohort in The Cancer Genome Atlas database and were randomly divided into a training set and an internal validation set at a ratio of 7∶3, and a total of 108 HCC patients from the GSE76427 dataset in the Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO) database were selected as the external validation set. The bulk RNA-sequencing data of tumor tissue and the corresponding adjacent normal tissue were collected from the above patients, as well as related clinical data including sex, age, tumor grade, and overall survival. Single-cell RNA-sequencing data of 4 HCC patients were obtained from the GSE162616 dataset of the GEO database. Dimensionality reduction and clustering were performed for single cells from the GSE162616 dataset, and ICD activity scores were calcula-ted for each type of cells. Cells were divided into high ICD activity group and low ICD activity group according to ICD activity score, and differentially expressed genes (DEGs) between the two groups were identified. Weighted gene co-expression network analysis (WGCNA) was performed for the RNA-sequencing data of 365 HCC patients in the LIHC cohort to identify the gene set with the strongest association with ICD, and this gene set was intersected with the DEGs between HCC tumor tissue and the corresponding adjacent normal tissue of the patients in the LIHC cohort, finally obtaining the HCC-specific ICD-related genes (ICDRGs). The univariate Cox regression analysis was used to identify prognosis-related ICDRGs among the above genes; 10 machine learning algorithms were integrated to select the optimal genes for constructing an ICD-related HCC prognostic risk score model; the Spearman correlation analysis was used to investigate the correlation between the expression levels of these optimal genes and immune cell infiltration in HCC tissue. The model was used to calculate the prognostic risk score for each patient in the training set, the internal validation set, and the external validation set, and each set of patients were divided into high- and low-risk groups based on the median score. The Kaplan-Meier (K-M) survival analysis, the receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve analysis, and the Cox regression analysis were used to evaluate the performance of the model in predicting prognosis. The univariate and multivariate Cox regression analyses were used to identify the influencing factors for the prognosis of HCC patients, which were integrated with the risk score to establish a nomogram model, and calibration curve and decision curve analysis (DCA) were used to assess the clinical translational value of the nomogram model. Results Five cell types were clustered in the GSE162616 dataset, among which immature B cells exhibited the highest ICD activity. A total of 920 ICD-related genes were identified at the single-cell level from the high and low ICD activity groups, and 82 HCC-specific ICDRGs were finally obtained through WGCNA. The univariate Cox regression analysis identified 26 prognosis-related ICDRGs from the above 82 genes, and after screening by machine learning algorithms, an ICD-related HCC prognostic risk score model was established based on 5 core genes, namely PIP4K2A, TAGLN2, RAC1, SLC16A3, and FYN. The Spearman correlation analysis showed that the expression levels of these 5 genes were significantly correlated with immune cell infiltration (P<0.001). The K-M survival analysis and the ROC curve analysis showed that this model had a good performance in predicting the prognosis of HCC patients, and the univariate and multivariate Cox regression analyses showed that the risk score of this model could be used as an independent predictive factor for the prognosis of HCC patients (HR=1.991,95%CI=1.368-2.898,P<0.001). The calibration curve showed that the nomogram model had a high accuracy in predicting the 1-, 3-, and 5-year survival rates of HCC patients, and DCA suggested that the nomogram model had the highest clinical benefit rate in terms of its performance in predicting 5-year survival rate. Conclusion The predictive model for the prognosis of HCC patients constructed in this study based on 5 HCC prognosis-related ICDRGs shows a good performance, and the nomogram model derived from the combination of this model and clinical indices also has a high clinical application value.

Supplementary tables for Genotypic, Environmental, and Symbiotic Drivers of Multi-Omics Data Variation in Reef- Building Corals: Towards a Point-of-Care Diagnostic Toolkit

Erin Chille, Debashish Bhattacharya — 2026-10-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21679334

Abstract of the Dissertation In response to the accelerating decline in coral cover worldwide, coral conservation and restoration efforts are rapidly expanding in size, scope, and geographic reach. Recent technical advances and decreasing costs associated with sequencing and liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry have made it possible to integrate information across ‘omics layers (e.g., genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, and metabolomics), allowing researchers to develop a systems-level understanding of the coral host and its associated microorganisms (the holobiont). Elucidating the genomic, microbiome-associated, and environmental underpinnings of coral health, as well as developing point-of-care tools for coral health monitoring are high-priority areas to assist in restoration and conservation efforts. Molecular biomarker-based point-of-care tools have yet to be widely implemented in the field. However, recent advances in biotechnology are reducing the costs and technical expertise required to integrate them into restoration projects and would allow coral restoration practitioners to make more informed, proactive decisions vis-à-vis their specific restoration program. Although specific biomarkers of coral stress have been identified, primarily comprising a suite of conserved cellular stress response genes, they are limited in diagnosing more specific population-and organismal-level health challenges faced by corals, such as disease and reproduction asynchrony, and lack large-scale validation across species, genotypes, symbiotic associates, and environments. In this dissertation, I examine how genetic, environmental, and symbiont-associated factors drive variation in coral stress responses at the transcriptomic and proteomic levels. In Chapter 1, I review the current state of coral health monitoring using molecular biomarker-based point-of-care tools. In Chapter 2, I investigate whether the outbreeding coral, Montipora capitata, and a primarily clonal population of Pocillopora acuta share a core transcriptomic response to thermal stress. Although a small number of differentially expressed genes were detected in response to a severe heat challenge global patterns of gene expression were primarily driven by genotype, ploidy, and G×E interactions within each species. In Chapter 3, I describe the generation of a publicly available, comprehensive multi-omics dataset comprising high-coverage whole-genome sequencing data for Montipora sp. 1 aff. capitata and untargeted, mass spectrometry-based bottom-up proteomic and metabolomic datasets for Montipora sp., Pocillopora spp., and Acropora spp. collected from ten reefs across Ulithi Atoll. In Chapter 4, I use these data to investigate the genotypic, environmental, and symbiont-associated drivers of global proteomic variation in Montipora sp. 1 aff. capitata and the effects of long-term, subsistence-level resource use on the coral symbiont community and host proteome. I find that human activity is associated with shifts in symbiont population size and taxonomic composition below the detection limit of ITS2 amplicon data. However, only ~2% of detected proteins significantly differed in abundance between reefs in Ulithi Atoll with high and low human activity. Instead, host proteomic variation was primarily attributed to site-level environmental heterogeneity. Lastly, in Chapter 5 I investigate the evolutionary origins of genes with a significant transcriptomic response to temperature-induced bleaching in the coral, Galaxea fascicularis. Approximately 96% of bleaching-responsive genes belonged to orthogroups inferred to have originated before the radiation of Cnidaria and 98% were annotated as a described ortholog. These genes were enriched for pathways typically implicated in bleaching, including amino acid and nitrogen metabolism, the cellular stress response, and the innate immune response. Overall, this work demonstrates that although the magnitude and timing of the coral molecular response to stress is strongly influenced by lineage, genotype, and local environmental conditions, there appears to be a conserved stress-response network that is shared across coral species. Beyond advancing knowledge of the genomic underpinnings of coral stress responses, my dissertation research provides publicly available resources and a framework for biomarker discovery and validation to support the development of molecular point-of-care tools for coral restoration and conservation.

Trends in kidney stone disease: a 5-year update of stone management in England and Scotland

Catherine E. Lovegrove, Robert Geraghty, Imran Ahmad, Hing Y. Leung, Ben Turney — 2026-10-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1111/bju.70409

Objectives: To describe contemporary trends in kidney stone disease (KSD)-related inpatient episodes, procedures, and costs in England and Scotland, with emphasis on post-coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) service recovery, socioeconomic variation in Scotland, comparison with other urological procedures, and 10-year healthcare demand and cost projections. Materials and Methods: Retrospective descriptive analyses were conducted using English Hospital Episode Statistics (2020–2025) and Public Health Scotland data (2019–2024). KSD-related finished consultant inpatient episodes and procedures were identified using the International Statistical Classification of Diseases and Related Health Problems 10th Revision and Operation and Procedures fourth edition codes, respectively. Procedures were categorised as extracorporeal shockwave lithotripsy, ureterorenoscopy (URS), percutaneous nephrolithotomy, or open. Trends were compared with uro-oncological resections and benign prostate obstruction procedures. Socioeconomic variation was assessed using the Scottish Index of Multiple Deprivation. The 10-year healthcare demand and cost projections, accounting for National Health Service (NHS) cost uplift factors, were modelled using historical data. Results: The COVID-19 pandemic disproportionately reduced KSD-associated activity compared with procedures for bladder and kidney cancer. Although volumes now exceed pre-pandemic levels, inpatient episodes remain below baseline and waiting times remain prolonged. Between 2020 and 2025, inpatient episodes increased by 27.4% in England and 17% in Scotland, with URS accounting for >50% of interventions. KSD procedural volumes are comparable to the combined volume of uro-oncological resections and benign prostatic obstruction procedures. In Scotland, 44% of procedures were performed in the two most deprived socioeconomic quintiles versus 37% in the two least deprived. In England, KSD-associated episodes are projected to increase by 33% to 111 907 (95% confidence interval 96 057–130 373) by 2034–2035, with an estimated cumulative NHS cost of £1.82 billion over the next decade. Conclusion: Kidney stone disease exacts a growing burden on healthcare services with incomplete recovery of inpatient care following the COVID-19 pandemic, socioeconomic disparities, and rising healthcare demand. These findings can inform service planning and prioritise evaluation of strategies to improve efficiency, equity, and prevention.

Artificial Intelligence For Climate-Resilient Plants: Emerging Ai Approaches For Predicting Drought, Salinity And Temperature Stress.

Aruna Nangare, Vaishali Wankhede — 2026-09-30 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22724439

Abstract Climate change is creating serious problems for agriculture by increasing drought, soil salinity, and extreme temperatures. These stresses affect plant growth, development, crop yield, and global food security. Traditional methods used to study plant responses to stress are often time-consuming, labour-intensive, and difficult to use for large numbers of plants. Artificial intelligence (AI) is becoming an important tool for studying and predicting plant responses to changing environmental conditions. AI can analyse large amounts of data collected through plant phenotyping, remote sensing, environmental sensors, and molecular studies. This review focuses on recent AI approaches used to predict plant responses to drought, salinity, and temperature stress from 2016 to 2026. Machine learning, deep learning, computer vision, thermal imaging, and hyperspectral imaging can help in early detection and prediction of plant stress. Recent developments are moving beyond simple stress identification towards predicting crop performance and stress tolerance. The combination of AI with high-throughput phenotyping and multi-omics can help identify stress-tolerant crop varieties and support climate-resilient breeding. However, challenges related to data quality, limited field validation, unclear model predictions, and poor performance across different environments still remain. Future research should develop reliable and explainable AI models for sustainable agriculture and improved crop production under climate change.

Bacteriële proteïne aggregaten als nieuwe tools in synthetisch biologische toepassingen

Ronald Van Eyken — 2026-09-28 — openalex

DOI : —

One of the currently outstanding challenges in synthetic biology (synbio) is to engineer a population of isogenic genetically modified micro-organisms towards an autonomous, multifunctional, multicellular community that allows to display more complex and diverse functionalities in relevant applications such as bioprocessing, bioremediation and microbial therapy. In this context, we have developed a novel tool to predictably engineer bacterial cell differentiation and population programming in E. coli as a model organism, based on intracellular bacterial protein aggregates that normally emerge under stress. These bacterial protein aggregates (PA) consist of misfolded proteins that form one large indivisible proteinaceous structure inside the cell, which is typically located at one of the two cell poles in rod-shaped bacteria. When this PA-bearing cell grows and divides, the PA is asymmetrically segregated among the two nascent daughter cells, thereby generating two phenotypically distinct PA-bearing and PA-free daughter cells. We leveraged this naturally occurring phenotypic heterogeneity for synthetic cell differentiation, by specifically attaching proteins with a desired functionality to a PA, so that these proteins can be exclusively allocated to the PA-bearing cell population. As such, PA-bearing and PA-free cells can be differentiated from one another in a highly predictable fashion. By fusing proteins with a desired functionality to a synthetic protein aggregation tag, we could transiently load the cells with this functionalized PA and subsequently let the inherent asymmetric segregation of the PA autonomously differentiate the population. We elaborated the functionality of this PA-based cell differentiation system in two proof-of-concept studies that focused either on engineering novel growth behavior in bacteria or on expanding the currently available bacterial population programming toolbox. In the first proof-of-concept study, we addressed the current need for stringent control of outgrowth and biocontainment of (therapeutic) genetically engineered bacteria within difficult to control environments, by fundamentally reprogramming their proliferation dynamics. We exclusively attached proteins that are essential for growth (producing an essential metabolite) to the PA so that the population could be differentiated in growing PA-bearing and non-growing PA-free cells. As such, we were able to exclusively endow the expansion of the population to the initial amount of PA-bearing grower cells, thereby changing native exponential proliferation dynamics into finite linear microbial expansion. Additionally, we modified the stability of the PA by tuning the cell's native disaggregation machinery, to predetermine for how many generations the PA will be able to provide the essential metabolite to the PA-bearing cell. As such, we built a novel PA-based biocontainment system that curbs growth to a slower, more controllable linear proliferation with a prefixed maximal expansion limit without the need for any external interventions, as the PA autonomously differentiates the population in grower and non-grower cells. In a second proof-of-concept study, we engineered an alternative and more universal strategy to impose cell differentiation in a bacterial population. By tagging repressor proteins with an aggregation tag and expressing them below the aggregation threshold, such proteins can remain functional within PA-free cells. In contrast, in PA-bearing cells, the aggregation-tagged repressor is attracted and relocated to the PA, thereby disrupting its native functional localization at its operator site on the chromosome and allowing transcription from the repressor's corresponding promotor. This phenomenon of PA-mediated derepression thus allows differential expression of a gene within PA-bearing and PA-free cells. In summary, we demonstrated that we can functionalize intracellular protein aggregates and exploit their naturally occurring asymmetry within bacterial populations to specifically allocate cellular functionalities to the PA-bearing cell, thereby differentiating a bacterial population in two distinct cell types. We successfully used this PA-based multicellular approach to engineer specific growth behavior and cell differentiation in E. coli, thereby establishing protein aggregates as a novel tool in synbio applications.

A Unified Framework of the Seonggil Field Equations and HJB Tensor PDEs for High-Dimensional Gene Regulatory Networks via scRNA-seq Integration

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Seonggil Lee — 2026-09-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22738129

This paper presents a unified geometric control framework for high-dimensional gene regulatory networks, bridging rigorous theoretical physics with practical Cellular Control Therapeutics. We integrate the Seonggil Field Equations with a stochastic Hamilton-Jacobi Bellman (HJB) tensor PDE on a Riemannian manifold. To directly apply this theoretical PDE to empirical single- cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) datasets, we overcome the destructive measurement limitations of scRNA-seq by replacing point-to-point trajectory tracking with a distribution-matching paradigm. Utilizing a Deep Forward-Backward StochasticDifferential Equation (FBSDE) architecture combined with a Multi-scale Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) Hybrid Loss, we enable the optimal transport and control of cellular populations towards healthy states. The complete theoretical derivation and its PyTorch pipeline are provided.

scRNA-seq Analysis Pipeline for Skeletal Muscle Regeneration Across Aging (Young/Old/Geriatric)

Chen Jie — 2026-09-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22738940

Overview This repository contains an R script (ageing_data_codes.R) implementing a full single-cell RNA-seq (scRNA-seq) analysis pipeline investigating how skeletal muscle regeneration changes across aging. Samples span three age groups — Young, Old, and Geriatric — profiled at four timepoints after injury: D0 (uninjured), D2, D5, and D7. The pipeline takes raw 10x Genomics CellRanger output through quality control, integration, clustering, cell-type annotation, targeted gene expression analysis, pseudobulk differential expression, and cell-cell communication (ligand-receptor) analysis. What the pipeline does Data import & QC Loads filtered_feature_bc_matrix.h5 (CellRanger) output per sample using Read10X_h5() / Read10X(). Creates a Seurat object per sample (min.cells = 10, min.features = 200). Computes mitochondrial (percent.mt) and ribosomal (percent.rb) content per cell. Filters low-quality cells and putative doublets (500 < nFeature_RNA < 5000, percent.mt < 10). Merging & integration Merges all per-sample objects into a single Seurat object (allcells). Adds metadata: sampleID, age_group (Young/Old/Geriatric), and timepoint (D0/D2/D5/D7). Normalizes (LogNormalize), finds variable features, scales (regressing out nCount_RNA and percent.mt), and runs PCA. Integrates samples with Harmony (IntegrateLayers) to correct for batch effects across samples. Runs UMAP, FindNeighbors, and FindClusters (resolution 0.2) on the Harmony-corrected embedding. Cell type annotation Clusters are annotated using canonical marker genes into: MuSCs and progenitors, FAPs, endothelial cells, macrophages, B cells, T cells, tenocytes, smooth muscle cells, neutrophils, glial cells, mature skeletal muscle, erythrocytes, and NK cells. Targeted gene expression analysis (Lancl1 / Lancl2) Plots Lancl1/Lancl2 expression split by age group and timepoint. Computes the percentage of cells positive for each gene, per cell type, age group, and timepoint, exporting summary tables (CSV/XLSX). Pseudobulk differential expression (DESeq2) Aggregates raw counts into pseudobulk samples (per cell type/sample). Runs DESeq2 contrasts: Day-wise comparisons within each age group (e.g., D2 vs D0, D5 vs D0, D7 vs D0) for MuSCs and for whole-muscle pseudobulk. Age-group comparisons at matched timepoints (Old vs Young, Geriatric vs Young, etc.). Produces result tables (CSV) and volcano plots (with ggrepel gene labeling and Lancl1/Lancl2 highlighting). Cell-cell communication analysis (CellChat) Subsets cells by timepoint/age group and builds CellChat objects using the mouse ligand-receptor database (CellChatDB.mouse). Computes communication probabilities and pathway-level signaling networks per timepoint. Merges CellChat objects across timepoints for comparison and centrality analysis. Generates circle plots, chord diagrams, heatmaps, and bubble plots of signaling pathways (e.g., WNT, TGFb, BMP, CCL, CXCL) between cell types. Requirements R (≥ 4.2 recommended) Key packages: Seurat (v5), harmony, dplyr, tidyverse, patchwork, ggplot2, hdf5r, RColorBrewer, cowplot, pals, viridis, shades, limma, dittoSeq, DESeq2, ggrepel, tibble, writexl, future, CellChat (jinworks/CellChat on GitHub), circlize, ComplexHeatmap, presto Install Bioconductor packages via BiocManager::install(...) and CellChat via devtools::install_github("jinworks/CellChat"). Input data The script expects per-sample, per-timepoint 10x Genomics CellRanger output (filtered_feature_bc_matrix.h5, or the raw matrix directories for some D7 samples) in the working directory, named by group/replicate/timepoint, e.g.: y1_day0_filtered_feature_bc_matrix.h5 # Young, replicate 1, Day 0 o2_day5_filtered_feature_bc_matrix.h5 # Old, replicate 2, Day 5 g3_day7_filtered_feature_bc_matrix.h5 # Geriatric, replicate 3, Day 7 Raw sequencing data / CellRanger outputs are not included in this deposit; the script assumes they are available locally or obtained from the associated dataset accession. Outputs Running the pipeline produces: An integrated, clustered, and annotated Seurat object (.rds) UMAP plots by sample, age group, and timepoint Marker-gene feature plots for cell-type annotation Lancl1/Lancl2 expression plots and summary tables (CSV/XLSX) Pseudobulk DESeq2 results tables and volcano plots (CSV/TIFF) CellChat cell-cell communication plots (circle, chord, heatmap, bubble)

Integrated Reanalysis of Childhood Infectious Diarrhea Whole-Blood Transcriptomes Defines Robust Pathway-Level Remodeling and Limited Regulatory Concordance

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Ahmad Ali, Mohammad Adil, Rakhshanda Rani, Hongsu He, Yuefeng Wu — 2026-09-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.20944/preprints202609.0987.v1

Background: Whole-blood transcriptomics captures systemic responses to infectious diarrhea, but pooled public-dataset reanalysis can be confounded by leukocyte composition and does not by itself establish cell-intrinsic regulation. Methods: We performed a profile-level secondary analysis of GSE69529 (255 profiles from 246 children; 203 diarrhea and 52 control profiles, including nine repeated profiles) using differential expression, functional enrichment, exploratory network analysis, promoter-sequence analysis, motif discovery, and transcription-factor activity inference. Because the pooled GEO2R contrast did not adjust for leukocyte composition, age, sex, pathogen, or repeated-subject structure, differentially expressed genes were interpreted as a composite whole-blood response. Published results from GSE276395 were used only for cross-study concordance, not as an independently reanalyzed validation dataset. Results: The pooled analysis identified 7,050 genes at adjusted P < 0.05 among 15,740 analyzed genes and highlighted broad immune, RNA-processing, cell-cycle, intracellular-organization, apoptosis, trafficking, and metabolic programs. Promoter composition was similar between regulatory directions; no HOCOMOCO motif was supported by AME, no STREME motif met the prespecified E-value threshold, and no CollecTRI-ULM transcription factor remained significant after false-discovery-rate correction. GSE276395 showed concordant innate-immune, neutrophil-centered, and immunometabolic themes, whereas its recurrent 12-gene set did not overlap the 12-gene discovery-stage panel. Conclusions: The reproducible signal is broad pathway-level whole-blood remodeling. Gene-, motif-, and regulator-level findings are exploratory, and the present design cannot distinguish shifts in leukocyte composition from within-cell transcriptional regulation.

Bone aging: a paradigm of multiscale degeneration and targeted rejuvenation

Kangpeng Li, Chenyuan Gao, Ti Zhang, Cheng Wang, Xiao Geng — 2026-09-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41413-026-00582-w

Abstract Bone aging is not merely a process of bone mass loss but rather a multi-level, networked degenerative phenomenon spanning molecular, cellular, tissue, and system levels. With the accelerating pace of global population aging, bone degenerative diseases have emerged as a major public health challenge. Consequently, there is an urgent need to elucidate the underlying mechanisms from an integrated perspective and to develop innovative intervention strategies. This review systematically synthesizes the comprehensive landscape of bone aging. At the macroscopic level, bone aging is characterized by declines in bone mass, microstructure, and biomechanical properties, ultimately leading to increased fragility. At the microscopic level, its pathogenesis involves dysfunction across multiple layers. Molecular mechanisms include genetic and epigenetic alterations, oxidative stress, the senescence-associated secretory phenotype, and dysregulation of key signaling pathways. Cellular level changes involve telomere attrition, mitochondrial and endoplasmic reticulum dysfunction, impaired proteostasis, altered nutrient sensing, and cellular senescence. At the microenvironmental and systemic levels, contributing factors include chronic inflammation, hormonal changes, and extracellular matrix remodeling. Correspondingly, intervention strategies include non-pharmacological approaches, such as dietary optimization, physical activity, and avoidance of alcohol and tobacco, as well as pharmacological therapies, including antiresorptive agents, anabolic bone-forming drugs, and senolytics. Emerging therapeutic avenues include regenerative medicine, gene therapy, and tissue engineering. Future research should focus on constructing a multi-omics-integrated bone aging atlas, developing bone organoids and computational models, establishing multimodal risk assessment systems, advancing precision therapies that target aging-related pathways, and promoting prevention-centered public health strategies. This review aims to provide a systematic framework for understanding the complex pathophysiology of bone aging and to provide key insights and directions for future interdisciplinary collaborations, translational research, and clinical interventions.

Preclinical toxicity and immunogenicity of a quadrivalent recombinant influenza hemagglutinin vaccine (SCVC101) in rhesus monkeys

Jianmin Guo, Cailing Dai, Yuankeng Huang, Jinlong Dai, Wenqiang Zhang — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vaccine.2026.129137

SCVC101 is a novel recombinant hemagglutinin (rHA) protein-based quadrivalent seasonal influenza vaccine containing HA gene sequences of A/Wisconsin/588/2019 (H1N1) pdm09-like virus, A/Cambodia/e0826360/2020 (H3N2)-like virus, B/Washington/02/2019 (B/Victoria lineage)-like virus, and B/Phuket/3073/2013 (B/Yamagata). Significant immune responses to influenza viruses induced by SCVC101 were demonstrated in mice, rats, and rhesus monkeys. This study aimed to investigate the toxicity and immunogenicity of SCVC101 in rhesus monkeys. Under Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) conditions, rhesus monkeys received intramuscular administrations of two distinct dose levels (15 μg and 45 μg of rHA per virus strain) during weeks 0, 3, and 6 of the experimental protocol. The results showed that SCVC101 vaccination elicited a strong specific binding antibody and neutralizing antibodies in the low- and high-dose vaccine groups. No significant systemic toxicities or abnormal cardiovascular and respiratory events were observed after three doses of SCVC101 in rhesus monkeys. Molecular mechanisms, epidemiology, and clinical implications of macrolide resistance in group A Streptococcus: a narrative review

Sun‐Joo Kim — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5145/acm.2026.29.3.11

Group A Streptococcus (GAS), or Streptococcus pyogenes, remains predictably susceptible to penicillin, whereas resistance to macrolides has become an important therapeutic and epidemiological concern. This narrative review summarizes the molecular mechanisms, epidemiology, clonal dissemination, and clinical implications of macrolide resistance in GAS. Macrolide resistance is mediated mainly by active efflux, commonly associated with mef genes and the M phenotype, and by ribosomal target-site methylation mediated by erm genes, which produces constitutive or inducible macrolide-lincosamide-streptogramin B resistance. Reported resistance rates vary markedly across geographic regions and study settings, from < 5% in some surveillance populations to > 90% in selected East Asian cohorts. However, direct comparisons are limited by differences in study period, patient population, infection type, and susceptibility testing methods. Molecular epidemiological and whole-genome sequencing studies indicate that both clonal expansion of successful emm lineages and horizontal transfer of resistance determinants carried by mobile genetic elements contribute to dissemination. Clinically, macrolide resistance may compromise treatment in patients requiring non-β-lactam therapy, while erm-mediated cross-resistance can limit the activity of clindamycin in invasive infections. Particular attention is required for inducible clindamycin resistance, which may be missed by routine susceptibility testing. GAS has not developed widespread clinically established penicillin resistance, although isolates with reduced in vitro β-lactam susceptibility associated with penicillin-binding protein alterations have been reported. Fluoroquinolone and tetracycline resistance remain less prominent but warrant continued surveillance. Integrated phenotypic and genomic surveillance, antimicrobial stewardship, and development of effective preventive strategies, including vaccines, are essential to monitor emerging resistant lineages and preserve therapeutic options.

Evaluation of the efficacy and duration of immunity of a bivalent subunit vaccine containing porcine circovirus type 2a and type 2d antigen

Troy Kaiser, Alyssa Jarrett, Wesley Johnson, Mark Hinds, Carmen Alonso — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vaccine.2026.129159

Porcine circovirus type 2 (PCV2) vaccination has been effective in protecting pigs from clinical disease and is used extensively today. Studies in vaccinated populations across the world indicate a major PCV2 genotype shift towards genotype 2d (PCV2d) becoming the predominant genotype, with genotype PCV2a still being a prevalent genotype. Here we present a series of studies that evaluated a novel commercial subunit vaccine, including both PCV2a and PCV2d antigens, Ingelvac CircoFLEX® AD, to protect pigs against PCV2 associated disease and provide homologous protection against PCV2a or PCV2d. Cesarean-derived, colostrum-deprived pigs were blocked by litter and randomly assigned to Placebo Control and Vaccine groups which received the novel vaccine. Six studies were carried out to determine efficacy at two weeks of age, the onset of immunity, and the duration of immunity after challenge with PCV2a or PCV2d. Across all six studies, 55 to 100% of Control pigs were positive for depletion and colonization while only 4 to 44% of Vaccine pigs were positive for depletion and colonization with statistically significant differences for each study, P ≤ 0.0031. These results support the efficacy of the novel vaccine against challenge by both PCV2a and PCV2d at 2 weeks of age, an onset of immunity of 2 weeks after vaccination, and 26 weeks duration of immunity.

Elucidating the role of pre-existing immunity in mucosal lymphoid architectural dynamics following live attenuated influenza vaccination

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Lukas Hoen, Sarah Lartey, Rishi D. Pathirana, Rebecca Jane Cox, Karl A. Brokstad — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vaccine.2026.129139

Mucosal immune responses in the upper respiratory tract are central to preventing influenza infection. The live attenuated influenza vaccine (LAIV) is delivered intranasally, making it a valuable model for investigating mucosal immunity in humans. In this study, we examined structural and cellular changes in palatine tonsils following LAIV vaccination in cohorts of children and adults undergoing tonsillectomy. Using quantitative immunofluorescence microscopy, we assessed germinal centre and mantle zone architecture over time and quantified the distribution of IgG- and IgA-positive cells within these compartments. We also developed an in-situ staining approach to detect hemagglutinin-binding cells in paraffin-embedded tonsillar tissue. Age-dependent differences in lymphoid architecture following LAIV vaccination were observed 3 days after vaccination in children; germinal centre sizes appear smaller and IgG and IgA frequency peaks. In contrast, in adults, germinal centre sizes and IgG and IgA frequency gradually increase with increasing time post-vaccination. Mantle zone areas in children and adults increased following LAIV. In addition, hemagglutinin-binding cells were detected in tonsillar tissue, and in some instances co-localized with IgA. Pre-existing immunity influences immune kinetics; our results suggest children rapidly generate de novo germinal centres and class-switched antibodies, in contrast to adults who may expand preexisting germinal centres and trend toward gradual increases over time.

Incidence of Pulmonary Vascular Thrombosis in Intensive Care Units Following COVID-19 Vaccination

Mir Mohammad Taghi Mortazavi, Mehdi khanbabayi Gol, Mozhdeh Nikfar — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22736966

Introduction: While COVID-19 vaccines substantially reduced global mortality, rare hypercoagulable safety signals such as immunothrombosis emerged. In critically ill ICU patients, pre-existing systemic inflammation and endothelial strain may compound these thrombotic risks within the delicate pulmonary vasculature. Consequently, this study aimed to evaluate the incidence and clinical characteristics of pulmonary vascular thrombosis in ICU patients following COVID-19 vaccination. Material and methods: This cross-sectional study was conducted at Imam Reza Center, Tabriz, recruiting 250 ICU patients selected via convenience sampling based on Cochran’s formula. Eligible post-vaccination patients were evaluated for pulmonary vascular thrombosis using CTPA. Demographic, clinical, hemodynamic, and laboratory data including D-dimer and anti-PF4 levels were abstracted from medical records and analyzed to identify clinical predictors. Results: Pulmonary vascular thrombosis significantly correlated with thrombocytopenia (61.8% vs. 17.6%, P<0.001), elevated D-dimer (6.84±3.42 vs. 2.22±1.28 mg/L FEU, P<0.001), and higher mPAP (31.40±8.24 vs. 22.33±4.98 mmHg, P<0.001). Multivariable regression identified anti-PF4 positivity (aOR:6.842, 95% CI:2.418–19.362, P<0.001), thrombocytopenia (aOR:3.428, 95% CI:1.584-7.418, P=0.002), and adenoviral vaccination (aOR:2.815, 95% CI:1.382-5.735, P=0.004) as independent predictors. Conclusion: Post-vaccination pulmonary vascular thrombosis in critically ill patients is independently driven by adenoviral vector platforms, thrombocytopenia, and anti-PF4 seropositivity, leading to marked hemodynamic compromise. Routine screening with D-dimer, platelet counts, and anti-PF4 antibody assays facilitates early risk stratification, timely intervention, and mitigated ICU mortality.

accines in Modern Public Health: Evidence-Based Efficacy, Safety Mechanisms, and Scientific Refutation of the Autism Myth

HENRY ATTAR — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22736724

This comprehensive research paper examines the safety, efficacy, and public health importance of vaccines in modern medicine. The paper provides an evidence-based analysis of vaccine mechanisms across multiple types (live attenuated, inactivated, mRNA, and subunit vaccines), supported by extensive epidemiological data demonstrating disease prevention across measles, polio, pertussis, and COVID-19. A significant section thoroughly refutes the fraudulent claim linking vaccines to autism, examining the retracted Wakefield study, analyzing large-scale epidemiological studies involving millions of subjects, and explaining why no biological mechanism supports this false assertion. The paper details rigorous vaccine safety monitoring systems (VAERS, VSD, CISA) and documents verified adverse effects within proper risk context. The paper demonstrates that vaccine-preventable diseases cause millions of deaths and disabilities annually, while serious vaccine adverse events occur at rates far lower than disease harms prevented. It addresses common vaccine hesitancy claims and provides scientific evidence supporting continued vaccination as essential to global public health.

A Spatial-Simulation-MCDM Framework for Prioritizing Vaccine Cold-Chain Resilience Strategies in Libya

Ibrahim Badi, Osama Serrar — 2026-09-13 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.31181/smeor41202760

Vaccine cold chains preserve vaccine potency across storage, transport, and service-delivery points. In geographically dispersed health systems, cold-chain planning requires spatial, operational, and strategic evidence because storage failure, transport delay, and demand pressure can affect service continuity. This study proposes a decision-support framework for prioritizing vaccine cold-chain resilience strategies in Libya. The framework integrates city-level spatial screening of twenty principal urban centers, Monte Carlo disruption simulation, and a BWM-TOPSIS model informed by a four-expert assessment. The analysis separates calculated spatial indicators, structured screening scores, and expert elicitation inputs, and reports the simulation parameters, BWM comparison vectors, TOPSIS scoring procedure, consistency results, comparative ranking checks, and rank-reversal tests. Results place southern and southeastern cities at the upper end of the cold-chain vulnerability ranking and identify preventive maintenance and spare parts, digital temperature monitoring, and route planning with emergency redistribution protocols as the leading resilience package. The framework offers a transparent and updateable basis for cold-chain planning in Libya and comparable settings.
